1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	This section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\text{eff}}$ collapse (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\S\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\S\ref{sec:remediation}).
5	
6	\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}
7	\label{sec:effective-k}
8	
9	Table~\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\text{eff}} = 1.20\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\kappa \in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\text{eff}}$ ($\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\kappa \geq 0.41$ (Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).

41	\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}
42	\label{sec:confirmation-bias}
43	
44	The trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\% of constraints on average ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| / |\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.
45	
46	The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to weighted majority voting (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering conclusion-referencing constraints (23\% of total) halves the effect ($+2.45$\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.). Confirmation bias generalizes across families: bias ratios of $4.97\times$ (Mistral), $3.93\times$ (Qwen-14B), and $2.88\times$ (LLaMA) all exceed $1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}), with the gradient aligning to regime boundaries. Error analysis reveals 64\% of joint SICA+SC errors predict \textsc{Unknown} for \textsc{True}/\textsc{False} problems, and 30\% are confident systematic errors where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer (Appendix~\ref{app:error-categorization}). Bias intensifies along reasoning steps: late-step constraints (step $\geq 7$) exhibit 27.2\% conclusion leakage vs.\ 22.0\% for early steps (Fisher $p = 3 \times 10^{-6}$), and SICA restricted to late-only constraints degrades by $-10.3$\,pp while early-only gains $+2.0$\,pp.
47	
48	
49	\subsection{Process-Level Independence}
50	\label{sec:process-kappa}
51	
52	Answer-level $\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:
53	\begin{equation}
54	\label{eq:process-sim}
55	\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \sum_{i < j} \cos(\mathbf{v}_i^q, \mathbf{v}_j^q),
56	\end{equation}
57	where $\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.
58	
59	\begin{figure}[t]
60	\centering
61	\includegraphics[width=0.85\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}
62	\vspace{-2mm}
63	\caption{Answer agreement vs.\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}
64	\label{fig:process-kappa}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}
68	\label{sec:diagnostics}
69	
70	Further diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\ref{app:extraction}--\ref{app:entropy}).
71	
72	
73	\subsection{Remediation Analysis}
74	\label{sec:remediation}
75	\label{sec:interventions}
76	
77	We test more than twenty-five remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and fifteen additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.
78	
79	\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\Delta = -1.1$\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\pm 1$\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \in \{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\}$) yields $\Delta = 0.00$\,pp.
80	
81	\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\Delta \approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\Delta = -9.31$\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\ref{app:remediation}).
82	
83	\paragraph{Additional directions.} Fifteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, Z3 strict-mode, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).
84	
85	\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\kappa \approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\,pp. All families cluster above $\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.
```

(4-seed mean $+1.47$\\,pp). Mistral-7B on LogiQA ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$) shows the same SC range is insufficient when the domain resists formalization (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}).\n\n\\paragraph{Type A (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%): confirmation bias.} All nine Type~A conditions (SC $\\geq 60\\%$, combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. The largest positive trend is LLaMA-3.1-8B on StrategyQA ($\\Delta = +2.00$\\,pp, $p = 0.424$, n.s.), while Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold logical examples ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.\n\n\\paragraph{Type C (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\\%$) shows $\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \\emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence testing.} TOST equivalence tests \\citep{lakens2017equivalence} with margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp establish equivalence for 4 of 15 non-degenerate conditions; 5 show genuine SICA--SC differences (4 negative, 1 positive n.s.); the remaining 6 are underpowered (Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control}\n\\label{sec:oracle}\n\nOracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: FOL from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA yields $+29.90$\\,pp on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($p < 10^{-13}$), $+14.22$\\,pp on Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO ($p = 2.0 \\times 10^{-7}$), and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 10^{-14}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\\% (ProofWriter) of problems. The MAX-SAT mechanism is sound; Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\\,pp) captures only 11.5\\% of its oracle ceiling, with ${\\sim}$88\\% lost to extraction quality.\n\n\n",

```text
530	\label{app:failure-modes}
531	\label{sec:failure-modes}
532	
533	The regime-dependent pattern in Table~\ref{tab:main_results} reflects three distinct failure modes of self-extracted symbolic constraints.
534	
535	\paragraph{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\geq$ $\sim$60\%).} Strong models produce consistent reasoning traces, and constraints extracted from those traces mirror the answer distribution. When most constraints support the same candidate, MAX-SAT excludes few clauses ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), the penalty term in Eq.~\ref{eq:score} vanishes, and the scoring function degenerates to weighted vote counting (Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). SICA and SC then produce identical predictions. The ablation in Appendix~\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically: on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$), MAX-SAT and count-only baselines agree on 803 of 804 predictions.
536	
537	\paragraph{Type B: non-formalizable domains.} LogiQA problems require pragmatic and contextual reasoning that resists clean FOL formalization. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA achieves an extraction rate of 86.4\%, but the resulting constraints have a contradiction rate of only 0.8\%: extracted formulas rarely conflict across traces, leaving MAX-SAT with no logical tension to exploit. Mistral-7B on LogiQA sits in the same SC range (51.00\%) yet produces $\Delta = +0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$); Qwen2.5-14B on the same domain yields $\Delta = -0.50$\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Neither model benefits, confirming that the right accuracy regime is necessary but not sufficient when the domain resists formalization.
538	
539	\paragraph{Type C: error amplification (SC $\leq$ $\sim$40\%).} When model accuracy is low, the majority of traces reach incorrect answers. Constraints extracted from wrong traces encode incorrect reasoning as formal rules. Z3 treats these rules as soft clauses with weight proportional to their frequency, so the solver preferentially satisfies the wrong constraints and penalizes traces that happen to reach the correct answer. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($\Delta = -2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$) demonstrates this effect: SICA converts a weak positive signal in trace diversity into a net negative by formalizing the errors.
540	
541	
542	\section{Cross-Model Confirmation Bias}
543	\label{app:cross-model-bias}
544	
```

732	\section{Extended Limitations}
733	\label{app:limitations-extended}
734	
735	Beyond the limitations discussed in the main text, several additional constraints bound our conclusions. Seven of our eleven model-domain conditions have $n \leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; power analysis shows that the minimum detectable effect exceeds 5\,pp for these conditions (Table~\ref{tab:power}), so they serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. Our extraction prompts use a single zero-shot template per domain; alternative prompting strategies (few-shot exemplars, chain-of-thought extraction, or iterative refinement) might yield higher-quality constraints, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural rather than prompt-specific. The constraint quality analysis (91.8\% discriminative rate, 39.2\% source-trace minority weight) covers Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO + ProofWriter ($N = 804$); other model-domain combinations may exhibit different characteristics. Constraint extraction uses $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$ while trace sampling uses $T = 0.7$; alternative extraction temperatures might yield different results. Although we prompt for FOL-style formalizations, extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers (\S\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}); whether models capable of genuine quantified FOL would yield higher discriminative rates is untested. Deduplication uses syntactic equivalence, so semantically equivalent constraints with different surface forms receive separate weights. The GSM8K pilot ($n = 30$) illustrates the constraint-absent regime and should not be interpreted as confirmatory evidence.
736

Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\\% (ProofWriter) of problems. The MAX-SAT mechanism is sound; Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\\,pp) captures only 11.5\\% of its oracle ceiling, with ${\\sim}$88\\% lost to extraction quality.\n\n\n",

        "lines": [
          " ",
          " This correlation manifests through a specific mechanism: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal.",
          " ",
          "-The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Confirmation bias generalizes across model families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral-7B), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen2.5-14B), and $2.88{\\times}^{\\dag}$ (LLaMA-3.1-8B) all significantly exceed $1.0$ ($^{\\dag}$based on 4/12 traces due to extraction failures; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}). The oracle ceiling (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms that 75.9\\% of the potential gain is lost to self-extraction quality.",
          "+The causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts symbolic constraints consistent with it. Constraints from majority-answer traces use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% of cross-answer pairs share zero Z3 variables), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all, and aggregation reduces to a weighted majority vote (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). {
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "60f8c745-3caf-40df-a416-ded3bd7d54b6",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Analysis}\n\\label{sec:analysis}\n\nThis section tests the three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): trace correlation and $K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), systematic confirmation bias with $\\text{BR} > 1$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}), process-level independence (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}), supporting diagnostics (\\S\\ref{sec:diagnostics}), and the inescapability of the mirage under twenty-plus same-model interventions (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}).\n\n\\subsection{Condorcet Effective-K}\n\\label{sec:effective-k}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:independence} reports Fleiss' $\\kappa$ and Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) across 15 conditions at $K = 12$. Among instruction-tuned models, $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82, yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} = 1.20\\text{--}3.62$. The four Mistral-7B FOLIO seeds produce $\\kappa \\in [0.41, 0.51]$, yet $\\Delta$ ranges from $+3.43$ to $-0.49$\\,pp---seed-level constraint quality, not independence alone, appears to influence the variation. Two conditions are jointly necessary for positive $\\Delta$: sufficient $K_{\\text{eff}}$ ($\\leq 1.30$ never improves) and sufficient SC accuracy ($K_{\\text{eff}} = 3.62$ with SC $= 39\\%$ produces significant degradation). Cross-model validation confirms universality: all four families produce $\\kappa \\geq 0.41$ (Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization}; Appendix~\\ref{app:cross-model-kappa}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Trace independence metrics ($K = 12$, sorted by $\\kappa$ descending). $\\kappa$: Fleiss' Kappa. $K_{\\text{eff}}$: Effective-K (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}). SC: Self-Consistency baseline. $\\Delta$: SICA $-$ SC (pp). {*}\\,$p < 0.05$; positive $\\Delta$ values do not reach significance. multi-T $= T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$, 4 traces each.}\n\\label{tab:independence}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llccrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nModel & Condition & $\\kappa$ & $K_{\\text{eff}}$ & SC (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen3 (think) & FOLIO & 0.91 & 1.09 & ${\\sim}$84 & ${\\sim}$0 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & LogiQA & 0.82 & 1.20 & 78.50 & $-$0.50 \\\\\nQwen3 & FOLIO & 0.77 & 1.26 & 84.80 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nQwen2.5-14B & FOLIO & 0.74 & 1.30 & 75.49 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nQwen2.5-7B & FOLIO & 0.66 & 1.46 & 75.98 & $+$0.49 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.3$) & FOLIO & 0.53 & 1.77 & 58.33 & $-$1.47 \\\\\nMistral-7B & LogiQA & 0.51 & 1.81 & 51.00 & $+$0.50 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s456) & FOLIO & 0.51 & 1.82 & 55.88 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & FOLIO & 0.50 & 1.84 & 55.88 & $+$3.43 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s123) & FOLIO & 0.49 & 1.88 & 54.41 & $+$2.94 \\\\\nMistral-7B (s789) & FOLIO & 0.41 & 2.17 & 54.41 & $-$0.49 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}0.5$) & FOLIO & 0.52 & 1.80 & 58.33 & $-$0.98 \\\\\nMistral-7B (multi-T) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 57.84 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B ($T{=}1.0$) & FOLIO & 0.48 & 1.91 & 55.39 & 0.00 \\\\\nMistral-7B & PW-D5 & 0.21 & 3.62 & 39.33 & $\\mathbf{-2.83}${*} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Symbolic Confirmation Bias}\n\\label{sec:confirmation-bias}\n\nThe trace correlation above manifests through a specific mechanism that explains \\emph{all three} regime failures: self-extracted constraints systematically confirm the model's majority answer regardless of correctness. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), when SICA errs, 83.3\\% of extracted constraints support the incorrect majority answer---a bias ratio of $4.97\\times$ (pooled raw ratio for Mistral-7B FOLIO default seed; $4.24\\times$ when MaxSAT-weighted; per-model ratios in Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) compared to correct problems. Consequently, 94\\% of SICA errors overlap with SC errors: symbolic aggregation adds no independent corrective signal. The MAX-SAT solver rejects only 3.4\\% of constraints on average ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| / |\\mathcal{C}|$, $p = 0.636$ between correct/incorrect), confirming degeneration (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Counterintuitively, jointly-wrong problems produce \\emph{more} constraints (41.9 vs.\\ 37.1 for correct): higher symbolic density amplifies rather than corrects bias.\n\nThe causal chain is: the model generates an answer, then extracts FOL consistent with it. Constraints use trace-specific variable names (56.4\\% zero cross-answer variable overlap), so MAX-SAT retains nearly all and aggregation reduces to weighted majority voting (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). A premise-only experiment isolates the bias source: filtering conclusion-referencing constraints (23\\% of total) halves the effect ($+2.45$\\,pp, $p = 0.359$, n.s.). Confirmation bias generalizes across families: bias ratios of $4.97\\times$ (Mistral), $3.93\\times$ (Qwen-14B), and $2.88\\times$ (LLaMA) all exceed $1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}), with the gradient aligning to regime boundaries. Error analysis reveals 64\\% of joint SICA+SC errors predict \\textsc{Unknown} for \\textsc{True}/\\textsc{False} problems, and 30\\% are confident systematic errors where all 12 traces agree on the wrong answer (Appendix~\\ref{app:error-categorization}). Bias intensifies along reasoning steps: late-step constraints (step $\\geq 7$) exhibit 27.2\\% conclusion leakage vs.\\ 22.0\\% for early steps (Fisher $p = 3 \\times 10^{-6}$), and SICA restricted to late-only constraints degrades by $-10.3$\\,pp while early-only gains $+2.0$\\,pp.\n\n\n\\subsection{Process-Level Independence}\n\\label{sec:process-kappa}\n\nAnswer-level $\\kappa$ may underestimate the independence bottleneck: two traces can follow nearly identical reasoning yet reach different final answers, causing $\\kappa$ to treat them as disagreeing. We define process similarity as the mean pairwise cosine similarity of TF-IDF trace representations:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:process-sim}\n\\text{ProcessSim}(q) = \\frac{2}{K(K{-}1)} \\sum_{i < j} \\cos(\\mathbf{v}_i^q, \\mathbf{v}_j^q),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\mathbf{v}_i^q$ is the TF-IDF vector of trace $t_i$ for question $q$. On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$), mean ProcessSim is $0.69$, far exceeding answer-level $\\kappa = 0.41$. The process--answer correlation is model-strength dependent: $r = -0.06$ ($p = 0.36$) on Mistral-7B, where answer diversity does not reflect reasoning diversity (Figure~\\ref{fig:process-kappa}), rising to $r = +0.24$ ($p < 0.001$) on Qwen2.5-14B. This pattern generalizes across four families (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}): the cosine--$\\kappa$ gap narrows from $0.23$ (Mistral) to $0.02$ (Qwen-14B), meaning answer-level $\\kappa$ already captures the bottleneck for strong models, while process-$\\kappa$ reveals hidden homogeneity in weaker models where answer diversity masks process convergence. In all families, interventions targeting answer diversity (temperature scaling, diverse prompting) cannot alter the underlying reasoning paths.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.85\\columnwidth]{figures/fig3_process_kappa_scatter.pdf}\n\\vspace{-2mm}\n\\caption{Answer agreement vs.\\ CoT cosine similarity (Mistral-7B, FOLIO, $n{=}204$). Flat regression ($r = -0.06$, $p = 0.36$); stronger models show significant positive correlation ($r = +0.24$, Qwen-14B; Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-process}).}\n\\label{fig:process-kappa}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Supporting Diagnostics}\n\\label{sec:diagnostics}\n\nFurther diagnostics---constraint ablation, discriminativeness, entropy decomposition, and hyperparameter sensitivity---confirm the mechanism (Appendices~\\ref{app:extraction}--\\ref{app:entropy}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Remediation Analysis}\n\\label{sec:remediation}\n\\label{sec:interventions}\n\nWe test more than twenty-five remediation strategies spanning prompt engineering, method-level redesign, and fifteen additional directions. All produce neutral or negative results.\n\n\\paragraph{Prompt-level interventions.} Nine strategies spanning six dimensions (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:remediation}) all fail: contrastive prompting reduces non-discriminative constraints but $\\Delta = -1.1$\\,pp; cross-model extraction yields accuracy within $\\pm 1$\\,pp; multi-temperature ($T \\in \\{0.3, 0.7, 1.2\\}$) yields $\\Delta = 0.00$\\,pp.\n\n\\paragraph{Method-level interventions.} ICG ($\\Delta \\approx 0$, $p = .206$), Contrastive ($\\Delta = -9.31$\\,pp, $p < 0.01$), and Debate ($\\Delta = -18.63$/$-23.00$\\,pp, $p < 0.001$) all fail; three of six conditions reach significance, all negative (Table~\\ref{tab:method_interventions}, Appendix~\\ref{app:remediation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Additional directions.} Fifteen further experiments---code verification, iterative refinement, logprob filtering, cross-model ensembles, premise-only filtering, DPP trace selection, Z3 strict-mode, and alternative objectives---all produce neutral-to-negative results (Appendix Table~\\ref{tab:additional_interventions}).\n\n\\paragraph{Independence-accuracy trade-off.} Table~\\ref{tab:kappa_generalization} (Appendix) compares $\\kappa$ across four aggregation methods and three model families. SC, Diverse-Prompt, and ICG cluster at $\\kappa \\approx 0.40$--$0.47$; Debate reaches $\\kappa = 0.15$ but collapses accuracy by $-18.6$\\,pp. All families cluster above $\\kappa = 0.40$, confirming a same-model ceiling: methods that increase independence destroy accuracy, and vice versa.",

    "originalFile": "\\paragraph{Scale and language.}\nAll models range from 7B to 32B parameters; whether the independence bottleneck persists at larger scales (70B+) or with reasoning-specialized models is untested. All datasets are English-language logical reasoning benchmarks with discrete answer spaces. We do not evaluate trained FOL extractors, external solvers with verified grounding, or multilingual settings. Oracle analysis is limited to FOLIO and ProofWriter (gold annotations available); extending to commonsense reasoning requires constructing comparable gold constraint sets. The cross-architecture NLI verifier (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) provides no benefit on LogiQA (combo $\\Delta \\leq +1$\\,pp, all $p > 0.5$), indicating that cross-architecture escape requires task-verifier alignment---NLI is effective only when the task naturally decomposes into premise-conclusion entailment. Even on compatible tasks, NLI provides no benefit when SC accuracy exceeds verifier standalone accuracy (Qwen3-14B ProofWriter: SC 85\\% vs.\\ DeBERTa 36\\%, $\\Delta \\leq +0.50$\\,pp, n.s.), confirming that cross-architecture escape requires verifier competitiveness, not merely architectural independence.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.}\nThe per-label complementarity analysis of the cross-architecture NLI result (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) involves small cell sizes (e.g., 42 \\textsc{False} problems, 69 \\textsc{Unknown} problems); per-label McNemar tests would be underpowered. The bias ratio metric ($4.97\\times$) was initially computed on a single condition (Mistral-7B FOLIO, $n = 204$); cross-model replication (Table~\\ref{tab:cross-model-bias}) confirms the pattern but is limited to FOLIO. Four-seed replication shows 2 of 4 seeds yielding null or negative results, and the intermediate-SC region is narrow (SC $\\approx$ 50--55\\%). The process-level similarity metric uses TF-IDF representations (a lower bound on true process similarity). Pairwise Pearson $r$ between traces (mean $0.625$, Mistral-7B) is monotonically related to Fleiss' $\\kappa$ across all four model families, supporting $\\kappa$ as a valid consensus proxy despite the simplifying assumptions of Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational cost trade-off.}\nSICA requires $4.5\\times$ the wall-clock time of SC (81.4 vs.\\ 17.7\\,min for 204 questions at $K{=}12$), with constraint extraction accounting for 76\\% of overhead (\\S\\ref{sec:exp-setup}). This $3.5\\times$ additional cost yields no significant accuracy gain on any condition, making the method impractical in its current form. Alternative prompting strategies (few-shot, chain-of-thought extraction) might improve extraction quality, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural. Additional limitations regarding power analysis and deduplication strategy are discussed in Appendix~\\ref{app:limitations-extended}.\n\n\\paragraph{Reproducibility.} All code, data, and experimental scripts will be released upon publication to facilitate replication of SICA, the oracle controls, and all remediation experiments.\n",

          " \\paragraph{Type A: confirmation bias (SC $\\geq$ $\\sim$60\\%).} Strong models produce consistent reasoning traces, and constraints extracted from those traces mirror the answer distribution. When most constraints support the same candidate, MAX-SAT excludes few clauses ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), the penalty term in Eq.~\\ref{eq:score} vanishes, and the scoring function degenerates to weighted vote counting (Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). SICA and SC then produce identical predictions. The ablation in Appendix~\\ref{app:ablation} confirms this empirically: on Qwen2.5-14B (FOLIO + ProofWriter, $n = 804$), MAX-SAT and count-only baselines agree on 803 of 804 predictions.",
          " ",
          "-\\paragraph{Type B: non-formalizable domains.} LogiQA problems require pragmatic and contextual reasoning that resists clean FOL formalization. Qwen2.5-14B on LogiQA achieves an extraction rate of 86.4\\%, but the resulting constraints have a contradiction rate of only 0.8\\%: extracted formulas rarely conflict across traces, leaving MAX-SAT with no logical tension to exploit. Mistral-7B on LogiQA sits in the same SC range (51.00\\%) yet produces $\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$); Qwen2.5-14B on the same domain yields $\\Delta = -0.50$\\,pp ($p = 1.0$). Neither model benefits, confirming that the right accuracy regime is necessary but not sufficient when the domain resists formalization.",
          "+\\paragraph{Type B: non-formalizable domains.} LogiQA problems require pragmatic and contextual reasoning that resists clean logical formalization. Neither model benefits, confirming that the right accuracy regime is necessary but not sufficient when the domain resists formalization.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Type C: error amplification (SC $\\leq$ $\\sim$40\\%).} When model accuracy is low, the majority of traces reach incorrect answers. Constraints extracted from wrong traces encode incorrect reasoning as formal rules. Z3 treats these rules as soft clauses with weight proportional to their frequency, so the solver preferentially satisfies the wrong constraints and penalizes traces that happen to reach the correct answer. Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$) demonstrates this effect: SICA converts a weak positive signal in trace diversity into a net negative by formalizing the errors.",

          " \\section{Extended Limitations}",
          " \\label{app:limitations-extended}",
          " ",
          "-Beyond the limitations discussed in the main text, several additional constraints bound our conclusions. Seven of our eleven model-domain conditions have $n \\leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; power analysis shows that the minimum detectable effect exceeds 5\\,pp for these conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:power}), so they serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. Our extraction prompts use a single zero-shot template per domain; alternative prompting strategies (few-shot exemplars, chain-of-thought extraction, or iterative refinement) might yield higher-quality constraints, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural rather than prompt-specific. The constraint quality analysis (91.8\\% discriminative rate, 39.2\\% source-trace minority weight) covers Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO + ProofWriter ($N = 804$); other model-domain combinations may exhibit different characteristics. Constraint extraction uses $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$ while trace sampling uses $T = 0.7$; alternative extraction temperatures might yield different results. Although we prompt for FOL-style formalizations, extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers (\\S\\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}); whether models capable of genuine quantified FOL would yield higher discriminative rates is untested. Deduplication uses syntactic equivalence, so semantically equivalent constraints with different surface forms receive separate weights. The GSM8K pilot ($n = 30$) illustrates the constraint-absent regime and should not be interpreted as confirmatory evidence.",
          "+Beyond the limitations discussed in the main text, several additional constraints bound our conclusions. Seven of our eleven model-domain conditions have $n \\leq 60$, providing insufficient statistical power for confirmatory claims; power analysis shows that the minimum detectable effect exceeds 5\\,pp for these conditions (Table~\\ref{tab:power}), so they serve as regime characterization rather than definitive tests. Our extraction prompts use a single zero-shot template per domain; alternative prompting strategies (few-shot exemplars, chain-of-thought extraction, or iterative refinement) might yield higher-quality constraints, though the oracle gap suggests the bottleneck is structural rather than prompt-specific. The constraint quality analysis (91.8\\% discriminative rate, 39.2\\% source-trace minority weight) covers Qwen2.5-14B on FOLIO + ProofWriter ($N = 804$); other model-domain combinations may exhibit different characteristics. Constraint extraction uses $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$ while trace sampling uses $T = 0.7$; alternative extraction temperatures might yield different results. Although we prompt for symbolic formalizations, extracted constraints are predominantly propositional ground clauses without quantifiers (\\S\\ref{sec:constraint-extraction}); whether models capable of genuine quantified reasoning would yield higher discriminative rates is untested. Deduplication uses syntactic equivalence, so semantically equivalent constraints with different surface forms receive separate weights. The GSM8K pilot ($n = 30$) illustrates the constraint-absent regime and should not be interpreted as confirmatory evidence."
        ]
      }
    ],

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Setup}\n\\label{sec:exp-setup}\n\nWe evaluate SICA against Self-Consistency (SC) across six models on three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations across ${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances. Beyond accuracy comparisons, we conduct diagnostic analyses of symbolic confirmation bias and process-level trace independence (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\paragraph{Models.} Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen3-14B (non-thinking mode)~\\citep{yang2024qwen25}, Mistral-7B-Instruct-v0.3~\\citep{jiang2023mistral}, LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct~\\citep{dubey2024llama3}, and QwQ-32B~\\citep{qwen2024qwq} (7B--32B parameters).\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.} FOLIO~\\citep{han2022folio} ($n{=}204$, with gold logical annotations), ProofWriter depth-5~\\citep{tafjord2021proofwriter} ($n{=}600$), LogiQA~\\citep{liu2020logiqa} ($n{=}200$). StrategyQA~\\citep{geva2021strategyqa} ($n{=}200$) serves as a domain-formalizability diagnostic. GSM8K~\\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k} ($n{=}30$) and AIME 2024 ($n{=}22$) probe the degenerate regime ($C_0 < 5\\%$).\n\n\\paragraph{Parameters.} $K{=}12$ traces at $T{=}0.7$; extraction at $T_{\\text{ext}}{=}0.3$. Z3 timeout: 10s. $\\alpha{=}0.5$. Statistical significance via McNemar exact test ($\\alpha{=}0.05$); 95\\% bootstrap CIs from 10,000 resamples for $n \\geq 200$.\n\n\\paragraph{Computational cost.} On Mistral-7B FOLIO ($K{=}12$), SICA takes $4.5{\\times}$ SC wall-clock time; constraint extraction ($K$ additional LLM calls) accounts for 76\\% of overhead, Z3 solving is negligible (${<}0.1\\%$).\n\n\n\\subsection{Main Results}\n\\label{sec:main-results}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:main_results} reports accuracy across all conditions sorted by SC baseline. We identify three empirical regimes (thresholds observation-driven, not theoretically derived). SICA effectiveness depends sharply on this accuracy regime.\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{SICA vs.\\ SC across all conditions sorted by SC accuracy. region.} Mistral-7B on FOLIO (SC $\\sim$50--55\\%) shows positive directional trends in 2 of 4 seeds ($\\Delta = +3.43$\\,pp, $p = 0.092$; $+2.94$\\,pp, $p = 0.070$); no seed reaches $p < 0.05$ (4-seed mean $+1.47$\\,pp). Seeds with identical SC baselines (55.88\\%) diverge ($+3.43$ vs.\\ $0.00$\\,pp), suggesting seed-level constraint quality matters (Appendix~\\ref{app:entropy}). Mistral-7B on LogiQA ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, $p = 1.0$) shows the same SC range is insufficient when the domain resists formalization (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}).\n\n\\paragraph{Type A (SC $\\geq$ 60\\%): confirmation bias.} All nine Type~A conditions (SC $\\geq 60\\%$, combined $N > 2{,}800$) produce no significant positive effects. The largest positive trend is LLaMA-3.1-8B on StrategyQA ($\\Delta = +2.00$\\,pp, $p = 0.424$, n.s.), while Qwen2.5-14B on ProofWriter shows significant degradation ($\\Delta = -2.50$\\,pp, $p = 0.011$). Even in-context learning with gold logical examples ($\\Delta = +0.50$\\,pp, n.s.) cannot break confirmation bias.\n\n\\paragraph{Type C (SC $\\leq$ 40\\%): error amplification.} Mistral-7B on ProofWriter ($n = 600$, SC $= 39.33\\%$) shows $\\Delta = -2.83$\\,pp ($p = 0.014$): SICA \\emph{significantly hurts} by formalizing incorrect reasoning.\n\n\\paragraph{Degenerate cases.} When $C_0 < 5\\%$, nearly all traces agree and SICA reduces to SC by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Equivalence testing.} TOST equivalence tests \\citep{lakens2017equivalence} with margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp establish equivalence for 4 of 15 non-degenerate conditions; 5 show genuine SICA--SC differences (4 negative, 1 positive n.s.); the remaining 6 are underpowered (Appendix~\\ref{app:tost}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Oracle Control}\n\\label{sec:oracle}\n\nOracle variants replace self-extracted constraints with gold annotations: logical formulae from FOLIO ($n = 204$) and DSL from ProofWriter ($n = 600$). Oracle-SICA yields $+29.90$\\,pp on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($p < 10^{-13}$), $+14.22$\\,pp on Qwen2.5-14B FOLIO ($p = 2.0 \\times 10^{-7}$), and $+11.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p < 10^{-14}$). Z3 agrees with gold labels on 95.1\\% (FOLIO) and 97.1\\% (ProofWriter) of problems. The MAX-SAT mechanism is sound; Mistral-7B's best self-extracted result ($+3.43$\\,pp) captures only 11.5\\% of its oracle ceiling, with ${\\sim}$88\\% lost to extraction quality.\n\n\n",

### Tool result

_source line 14579 · 2026-05-20T20:17:17.116Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011wWp1nhPM88ZbHmo6VraFj`</summary>

```text
1	Self-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.
2	We investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts symbolic constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.
3	Across the full LLM capability range---from Mistral-7B (54\% SC) to Qwen3-14B thinking mode (86\% SC)---and three reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints do not improve majority voting at any capacity level: none of sixteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect; TOST equivalence tests (margin $\pm 2$\,pp) establish equivalence for four conditions, while five show genuine SICA--SC differences---four of which are negative.
4	We identify \emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\times$), and 94\% of errors overlap with SC.
5	Process-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.
6	Twenty-plus same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\kappa \geq 0.40$ across three model families).
7	Oracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\,pp and $+14.22$\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\sim}$12\% of the oracle ceiling.
8	However, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---yields $+3.50$\,pp on ProofWriter ($p = 0.013$; $+10.67$\,pp at $w{=}5$, $p < 0.001$), confirmed across three NLI architectures, suggesting that the oracle gap is partially recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution.
9	
```

```text
1	\section{Background \& Setup}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}
5	\label{sec:condorcet}
6	
7	Self-Consistency (SC) \citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \to \infty$ \citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\kappa$ \citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:
8	\begin{equation}
9	\label{eq:effk}
10	K_{\text{eff}} = \frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \cdot \kappa}.
11	\end{equation}
12	In our experiments (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\text{eff}} \approx 1.2\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. Note that $\kappa$ is a qualitative proxy for the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC); we use $K_{\text{eff}}$ as an interpretive indicator rather than a precise statistical estimator. This \emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.
13	
14	\subsection{SICA Pipeline}
15	\label{sec:pipeline}
16	
17	Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\ref{fig:pipeline}).
18	
19	\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\{t_1, \ldots, t_K\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.
20	
21	\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\ref{app:prompts}). The \emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.
22	
23	\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:
24	\begin{equation}
25	\label{eq:maxsat}
26	\mathcal{C}^* = \arg\max_{\mathcal{S} \subseteq \mathcal{C}} \sum_{c \in \mathcal{S}} w_c \quad \text{s.t.} \quad \mathcal{S} \text{ is satisfiable.}
27	\end{equation}
28	Z3 \citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\texttt{answer\_a} == \texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.
29	
30	\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\mathcal{C}^{\setminus} = \mathcal{C} \setminus \mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:
31	\begin{equation}
32	\label{eq:score}
33	\text{score}(a) = \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^*} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a) - \alpha \sum_{c \in \mathcal{C}^{\setminus}} w_c \cdot \sigma(c, a),
34	\end{equation}
35	where $\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \arg\max_{a} \text{score}(a)$.
36	
37	\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\mathcal{C}^{\setminus}| \approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:
38	\begin{equation}
39	\label{eq:degenerate}
40	\text{score}(a) \approx \sum_{\substack{c \in \mathcal{C}^* \\ \text{source}(c) \to a}} w_c,
41	\end{equation}
42	equivalent to majority voting. We show in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.
43	
44	\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\{q : |\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\}| / |\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC. The penalty weight $\alpha = 0.5$ is not a critical hyperparameter: accuracy varies by at most $0.49$\,pp across $\alpha \in \{0.0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\}$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO.
45	
46	\begin{figure}[t]
47	\centering
48	\small
49	\begin{tikzpicture}[
50	    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,
51	    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,
52	                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\scriptsize},
53	    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},
54	    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},
55	    lbl/.style={font=\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},
56	    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,
57	                    inner sep=3pt},
58	]
59	\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};
60	\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\traces};
61	\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\constraints};
62	\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\ +\\weight};
63	\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\(Z3)};
64	
65	\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};
66	\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\vote};
67	
68	\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);
69	\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);
70	\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);
71	\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);
72	\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);
73	\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);
74	
75	\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};
76	
77	\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\scriptsize\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};
78	
79	\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};
80	\end{tikzpicture}
81	\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}
82	\label{fig:pipeline}
83	\end{figure}
84	
85	\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}
86	\label{sec:mirage}
87	
88	We hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\S\ref{sec:oracle}). We quantify mirage intensity via the \emph{Bias Ratio}:
89	\begin{equation}
90	\label{eq:mirage}
91	\text{BR}(M,T) = \frac{P(c \text{ supports } a_M \mid a_M \neq a^*)}{P(c \text{ supports } a^* \mid a_M \neq a^*)},
92	\end{equation}
93	where $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\text{eff}} < 2$ (\emph{direct}; Eq.~\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\ref{eq:degenerate}). Together with the oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}), BR bounds improvement: self-extracted constraints capture at most a $1/\text{BR}$ fraction of the oracle ceiling ($\Delta_{\max} \lesssim 6.0$\,pp for Mistral-7B, $\lesssim 3.6$\,pp for Qwen-14B; observed mean across conditions: $+2.12$\,pp). Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below $1.0$ (\S\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\S\ref{sec:cross-arch}).
94

The formalization step thus provides a veneer of rigor without escaping the faithfulness problem.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Background \\& Setup}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Self-Consistency and the Condorcet Independence Assumption}\n\\label{sec:condorcet}\n\nSelf-Consistency (SC) \\citep{wang2023sc} samples $K$ reasoning traces and selects the majority answer. The Condorcet Jury Theorem (CJT) \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees that if $K$ independent voters are each correct with probability $p > 0.5$, the majority converges to truth as $K \\to \\infty$ \\citep{austensmith1996}. LLM traces violate this independence: all $K$ samples come from the same model with only temperature as variation. Following \\citet{ladha1992correlated} and the design-effect framework of \\citet{kish1965survey}, we define effective sample size using Fleiss' $\\kappa$ \\citep{fleiss1971kappa} as proxy for intraclass correlation:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:effk}\nK_{\\text{eff}} = \\frac{K}{1 + (K - 1) \\cdot \\kappa}.\n\\end{equation}\nIn our experiments (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}), $\\kappa$ ranges from 0.21 to 0.82 across instruction-tuned pairs (0.91 for thinking mode), yielding $K_{\\text{eff}} \\approx 1.2\\text{--}3.6$ for $K = 12$. Note that $\\kappa$ is a qualitative proxy for the intraclass correlation coefficient (ICC); we use $K_{\\text{eff}}$ as an interpretive indicator rather than a precise statistical estimator. This \\emph{independence bottleneck} motivates our investigation.\n\n\\subsection{SICA Pipeline}\n\\label{sec:pipeline}\n\nSymbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA) extends SC by extracting formal constraints from traces and selecting the answer best supported by the satisfiable subset (Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline}).\n\n\\paragraph{Trace generation.} Given question $q$, an LLM generates $K$ traces $\\{t_1, \\ldots, t_K\\}$ at temperature $T = 0.7$. Each trace $t_i$ yields answer $a_i$.\n\n\\paragraph{Constraint extraction.}\\label{sec:constraint-extraction} The same LLM extracts FOL constraints from each trace at $T_{\\text{ext}} = 0.3$. The extraction prompt requests JSON output with constraint type, expression, and Z3-compatible formula (Appendix~\\ref{app:prompts}). The \\emph{extraction rate} is the fraction of traces yielding at least one parseable constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Deduplication and MAX-SAT.} Constraints are deduplicated by syntactic equivalence; each unique constraint $c_m$ receives weight $w_m$ equal to the number of source traces. The objective finds the largest-weight satisfiable subset:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:maxsat}\n\\mathcal{C}^* = \\arg\\max_{\\mathcal{S} \\subseteq \\mathcal{C}} \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{S}} w_c \\quad \\text{s.t.} \\quad \\mathcal{S} \\text{ is satisfiable.}\n\\end{equation}\nZ3 \\citep{demoura2008z3} solves this with a 10-second timeout. Answer assertion constraints ($\\texttt{answer\\_a} == \\texttt{True}$, weight 1) ensure every candidate receives a nonzero base score, making SICA a strict generalization of SC.\n\n\\paragraph{Scoring.} Let $\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus} = \\mathcal{C} \\setminus \\mathcal{C}^*$. Each candidate $a$ is scored by:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:score}\n\\text{score}(a) = \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^*} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a) - \\alpha \\sum_{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}} w_c \\cdot \\sigma(c, a),\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\sigma(c, a) = 1$ if $c$ was extracted from any trace producing answer $a$, and $\\alpha = 0.5$. The selected answer is $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a} \\text{score}(a)$.\n\n\\paragraph{Degeneration condition.} If self-extracted constraints are coupled to their source answer, MAX-SAT retains nearly all ($|\\mathcal{C}^{\\setminus}| \\approx 0$), and scoring degenerates to:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:degenerate}\n\\text{score}(a) \\approx \\sum_{\\substack{c \\in \\mathcal{C}^* \\\\ \\text{source}(c) \\to a}} w_c,\n\\end{equation}\nequivalent to majority voting. We show in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias} that this degeneration occurs in practice.\n\n\\paragraph{The $C_0$ diagnostic.} The multi-candidate rate $C_0 = |\\{q : |\\mathcal{A}(q)| > 1\\}| / |\\mathcal{Q}|$ is a zero-cost diagnostic computable before extraction. When $C_0 < 5\\%$, SICA produces the same output as SC. The penalty weight $\\alpha = 0.5$ is not a critical hyperparameter: accuracy varies by at most $0.49$\\,pp across $\\alpha \\in \\{0.0, 0.1, 0.3, 0.5, 0.7, 1.0\\}$ on Mistral-7B FOLIO.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tikzpicture}[\n    node distance=0.4cm and 0.35cm,\n    box/.style={draw, rounded corners=2pt, minimum height=0.6cm,\n                text width=1.6cm, align=center, font=\\scriptsize},\n    arr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick},\n    darr/.style={-{Stealth[length=4pt]}, thick, densely dashed},\n    lbl/.style={font=\\scriptsize, text=gray!70!black},\n    ibottle/.style={draw=red!70!black, densely dashed, rounded corners=3pt,\n                    inner sep=3pt},\n]\n\\node[box] (q) {Question $q$};\n\\node[box, right=of q] (sample) {Sample $K$\\\\traces};\n\\node[box, right=of sample] (extract) {Extract FOL\\\\constraints};\n\\node[box, right=of extract] (dedup) {Dedup.\\ +\\\\weight};\n\\node[box, right=of dedup] (maxsat) {MAX-SAT\\\\(Z3)};\n\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of maxsat, fill=gray!12] (ans) {Answer $a^*$};\n\\node[box, below=0.7cm of sample, fill=gray!12] (sc) {Majority\\\\vote};\n\n\\draw[arr] (q) -- (sample);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- (extract);\n\\draw[arr] (extract) -- (dedup);\n\\draw[arr] (dedup) -- (maxsat);\n\\draw[arr] (maxsat) -- (ans);\n\\draw[arr] (sample) -- node[lbl, right, yshift=0pt] {SC} (sc);\n\n\\node[lbl, above=0.01cm of maxsat] {SICA};\n\n\\node[ibottle, fit=(sample)(extract), label={[font=\\scriptsize\\itshape, text=red!70!black]above:Independence Bottleneck}] {};\n\n\\draw[darr, gray!60] ([xshift=-0.1cm]dedup.south) -- ++(0,-0.35) node[lbl, below, yshift=-0.05cm] {oracle: gold FOL};\n\\end{tikzpicture}\n\\caption{The SICA pipeline. SC selects the majority answer from $K$ traces. SICA extracts FOL constraints, deduplicates, and solves MAX-SAT via Z3. Red dashed box: the independence bottleneck. Dashed arrow: oracle variant with gold FOL.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{The Symbolic Mirage Hypothesis}\n\\label{sec:mirage}\n\nWe hypothesize that self-extracted symbolic structures are not independent verification signals but projections of the generating model's distributional biases. This \\textbf{Symbolic Mirage} is specific to self-extracted constraints; symbolic methods employing independently sourced constraints remain effective (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}). We quantify mirage intensity via the \\emph{Bias Ratio}:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mirage}\n\\text{BR}(M,T) = \\frac{P(c \\text{ supports } a_M \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)}{P(c \\text{ supports } a^* \\mid a_M \\neq a^*)},\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $c$ is a self-extracted constraint, $a_M$ the model's answer, and $a^*$ the ground truth. $\\text{BR} > 1$ indicates constraints project model bias rather than provide independent verification. The mirage degrades aggregation through two channels: correlated constraints compress $K_{\\text{eff}} < 2$ (\\emph{direct}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}), and answer-coupled constraints collapse MAX-SAT to vote counting (\\emph{indirect}; Eq.~\\ref{eq:degenerate}). Together with the oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}), BR bounds improvement: self-extracted constraints capture at most a $1/\\text{BR}$ fraction of the oracle ceiling ($\\Delta_{\\max} \\lesssim 6.0$\\,pp for Mistral-7B, $\\lesssim 3.6$\\,pp for Qwen-14B; observed mean across conditions: $+2.12$\\,pp). Three testable predictions follow: (1)~BR $> 1.0$ across architectures (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}); (2)~no same-model intervention reduces BR below $1.0$ (\\S\\ref{sec:remediation}); (3)~only external verification escapes the mirage (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}).\n",

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related-work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Self-Consistency and extensions.}
5	SC selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions include universal SC for free-form generation \citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. \citet{loo2025reevaluating} show that SC gains plateau early. Our Condorcet analysis explains this: $\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ compresses $K_{\text{eff}}$ to 1.20--3.62 for $K = 12$ (\S\ref{sec:effective-k}).
6	
7	\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}
8	Logic-LM \citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \citep{olausson2023linc} translate problems into formal logic for external provers; LINC documents ${\sim}$40\% translation errors. Recent extensions---CLOVER \citep{ryu2025clover}, DyLoS \citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \citep{wang2025fover}, VERGE \citep{singh2026verge}, LogicAgent \citep{luo2025logicagent}---add compositional translation, solver selection, decomposition, trained verifiers, and formal refinement, each introducing capabilities beyond the reasoning model. \citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show that LLM formalization degrades with complexity; \citet{chan2026position} argue that logical soundness is not a reliable criterion for neurosymbolic verification with LLMs. Our oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound ($+29.90$/$+14.22$\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\,pp ProofWriter); the failure localizes to self-extraction quality (\S\ref{sec:oracle}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Test-time compute and verification.}
11	Scaling test-time computation through additional traces \citep{snell2025scaling} faces the same correlation challenge: same-model attempts collapse into correlated solutions. Reasoning models (o1, DeepSeek-R1 \citep{guo2025deepseekr1}, QwQ \citep{qwen2024qwq}) extend thinking time but remain subject to the independence bottleneck: QwQ-32B produces $\kappa = 0.91$ and $C_0 = 2.6\%$, suggesting that longer reasoning chains intensify homogenization rather than alleviating it. Trained verifiers offer an alternative \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd}; multi-agent debate refines answers through discussion \citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}. Self-correction without external feedback degrades performance \citep{huang2024selfcorrect, stechly2025selfverification}, paralleling our confirmation bias finding (\S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).
12	
13	\paragraph{CJT and ensemble diversity.}
14	CJT \citep{condorcet1785} guarantees convergence under independent voting; \citet{ladha1992correlated} prove correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size; \citet{lefort2024condorcet_llm} apply CJT to LLM classification. We extend this by quantifying \emph{why} self-correction fails---same-model traces are correlated voters ($K_{\text{eff}}$ collapses)---and showing this persists even under formal verification (Z3-checked symbolic constraints) and at the process level (\S\ref{sec:process-kappa}).
```

      "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related-work}\n\n\\paragraph{Self-Consistency and extensions.}\nSC selects the most frequent answer across chain-of-thought traces \\citep{wang2023sc}. Extensions include universal SC for free-form generation \\citep{chen2024usc}, confidence weighting \\citep{taubenfeld2025cisc}, diverse prompting with step-aware verification \\citep{li2023diverse}, program-based vote filtering \\citep{toh2024notallvotes}, and reasoning-aware filtering \\citep{wan2024rasc, kim2026reasc}. \\citet{loo2025reevaluating} show that SC gains plateau early. Our Condorcet analysis explains this: $\\kappa = 0.21$--$0.82$ compresses $K_{\\text{eff}}$ to 1.20--3.62 for $K = 12$ (\\S\\ref{sec:effective-k}).\n\n\\paragraph{Neuro-symbolic reasoning.}\nLogic-LM \\citep{pan2023logiclm} and LINC \\citep{olausson2023linc} translate problems into formal logic for external provers; LINC documents ${\\sim}$40\\% translation errors. Recent extensions---CLOVER \\citep{ryu2025clover}, DyLoS \\citep{xu2026dylos}, Aristotle \\citep{li2025aristotle}, FoVer \\citep{wang2025fover}, VERGE \\citep{singh2026verge}, LogicAgent \\citep{luo2025logicagent}---add compositional translation, solver selection, decomposition, trained verifiers, and formal refinement, each introducing capabilities beyond the reasoning model. \\citet{amonkar2025realitycheck} show that LLM formalization degrades with complexity; \\citet{chan2026position} argue that logical soundness is not a reliable criterion for neurosymbolic verification with LLMs. Our oracle controls confirm the mechanism is sound ($+29.90$/$+14.22$\\,pp FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter); the failure localizes to self-extraction quality (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}).\n\n\\paragraph{Test-time compute and verification.}\nScaling test-time computation through additional traces \\citep{snell2025scaling} faces the same correlation challenge: same-model attempts collapse into correlated solutions. Reasoning models (o1, DeepSeek-R1 \\citep{guo2025deepseekr1}, QwQ \\citep{qwen2024qwq}) extend thinking time but remain subject to the independence bottleneck: QwQ-32B produces $\\kappa = 0.91$ and $C_0 = 2.6\\%$, suggesting that longer reasoning chains intensify homogenization rather than alleviating it. Trained verifiers offer an alternative \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k, lightman2024prm, wang2024mathshepherd}; multi-agent debate refines answers through discussion \\citep{liang2023encouraging, du2023improving}. Self-correction without external feedback degrades performance \\citep{huang2024selfcorrect, stechly2025selfverification}, paralleling our confirmation bias finding (\\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}).\n\n\\paragraph{CJT and ensemble diversity.}\nCJT \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} prove correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size; \\citet{lefort2024condorcet_llm} apply CJT to LLM classification. We extend this by quantifying \\emph{why} self-correction fails---same-model traces are correlated voters ($K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapses)---and showing this persists even under formal verification (Z3-checked symbolic constraints) and at the process level (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).",
      "numLines": 14,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 14
    }
  },

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{CJT and ensemble diversity.}\nCJT \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} prove correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size; \\citet{lefort2024condorcet_llm} apply CJT to LLM classification. We extend this by quantifying \\emph{why} self-correction fails---same-model traces are correlated voters ($K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapses)---and showing this persists even under formal verification (Z3-checked symbolic constraints) and at the process level (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{CJT and ensemble diversity.}\nCJT \\citep{condorcet1785} guarantees convergence under independent voting; \\citet{ladha1992correlated} prove correlated voters reduce effective ensemble size; \\citet{lefort2024condorcet_llm} apply CJT to LLM classification. We extend this by quantifying \\emph{why} self-correction fails---same-model traces are correlated voters ($K_{\\text{eff}}$ collapses)---and showing this persists even under formal verification (Z3-checked symbolic constraints) and at the process level (\\S\\ref{sec:process-kappa}).\n\n\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nPrior self-correction studies \\citep{huang2024selfcorrect, stechly2025selfverification} establish qualitative negative results; we contribute reusable quantitative diagnostics that characterize the failure mode. Effective-$K$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:effk}) measures how much independent information $K$ traces actually carry. The bias ratio ($\\text{BR}$; Eq.~\\ref{eq:mirage}) quantifies the degree to which self-extracted constraints post-hoc rationalize the model's answer rather than independently verify it, connecting symbolic extraction to CoT faithfulness concerns \\citep{turpin2024language}. Conclusion leakage rate ($C_D$) isolates answer-dependent constraint contamination at the step level. Together, these diagnostics show that formal Z3-checked verification inherits the same bias ($\\text{BR} = 4.97\\times$)---a finding invisible to prior work that examines only natural-language self-correction. The cross-architecture escape (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-arch}) yields a capability gap corollary: external verification helps only when the verifier's standalone accuracy exceeds the generator's SC baseline, providing a practical decision criterion absent from existing frameworks."
}
```

```text
1	\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}
2	\label{sec:solutions}
3	\label{sec:cross-arch}
4	
5	The analyses in \S\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\S\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\S\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias. The oracle gap (\S\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution.
6	
7	\paragraph{Method.}
8	We test this with DeBERTa-large \citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \citep{williams2018mnli}, classifying (premise, conclusion) pairs into \textsc{entailment}, \textsc{contradiction}, or \textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via weight $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.
9	
10	\paragraph{Main result.}
11	DeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\%) and 55.00\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode yields $+1.96$\,pp at $w{=}1$ and $+2.94$\,pp at $w{=}3$ (both n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\,pp ($p = 0.013$) and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\,pp ($p < 0.001$)---while same-model SICA \emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). We report $w{=}3$ as primary; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$); FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. Per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\%) and performs poorly on \textsc{Unknown} (3.5\% accuracy), as NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.
12	
13	\paragraph{Why it works.}
14	The 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\S\ref{sec:mirage}). This produces per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \textsc{False} problems (71.43\% vs.\ SC's 38.10\%) where the LLM is weakest, while SC dominates \textsc{Unknown} (68.12\% vs.\ 18.84\%). All three NLI architectures tested---DeBERTa, RoBERTa, and BART (all MNLI-trained)---produce significant improvements on ProofWriter at $w \geq 3$ (Appendix~\ref{app:multi-nli}), confirming the effect is not verifier-specific.
15	
16	\paragraph{Capability gap hypothesis.}
17	Cross-architecture verification succeeds when the verifier's standalone accuracy exceeds the generator's SC baseline. On ProofWriter, DeBERTa standalone (55.00\%) exceeds Mistral-7B SC (39.33\%), enabling $+10.67$\,pp at $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$). On Qwen3-14B FOLIO, where SC (86.27\%) far exceeds DeBERTa (49.51\%), the combo yields $\Delta \leq +0.49$\,pp (all $p = 1.0$). Aggregation gain shrinks with model capability: $+8.01$\,pp (Mistral-7B) to $+0.86$\,pp (Qwen3-14B thinking mode; Appendix~\ref{app:single-trace}). Across all conditions at $w{=}3$, combo gain tracks the capability gap (Figure~\ref{fig:capability-gap}): meaningful improvement emerges only when the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's SC baseline.
18	
19	\begin{figure}[t]
20	\centering
21	\includegraphics[width=0.82\columnwidth]{figures/capability_gap_scatter}
22	\vspace{-3mm}
23	\caption{Combo gain vs.\ capability gap (verifier standalone $-$ generator SC) at $w{=}3$. Meaningful gain emerges when the gap is positive (verifier exceeds generator).}
24	\label{fig:capability-gap}
25	\vspace{-6mm}
26	\end{figure}
27	
```

Stand.\\ = NLI standalone accuracy (\\%). \\textbf{Bold $\\Delta$}: $p < 0.05$.}\n\\label{tab:cross-generator-nli}\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llrrrr@{}}\n\\toprule\nGenerator & NLI Verifier & SC (\\%) & Stand.\\ (\\%) & $\\Delta$ (pp) & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nMistral-7B & DeBERTa-base & 39.33 & 55.00 & $\\mathbf{+10.67}$ & ${<}.001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B & BART-large & 39.33 & 46.20 & $\\mathbf{+7.17}$ & ${<}.001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B & RoBERTa-large & 39.33 & 37.70 & $\\mathbf{+6.50}$ & ${<}.001$ \\\\\nMistral-7B & DeBERTa-v3-lg & 39.33 & 45.17 & $+4.83$ & .110 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nQwen3-14B & DeBERTa-base & 85.00 & 36.17 & $+0.33$ & .830 \\\\\nQwen3-14B & DeBERTa-v3-lg & 85.00 & 45.17 & $-0.67$ & .800 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Cross-Architecture Proof-of-Concept}\n\\label{sec:solutions}\n\\label{sec:cross-arch}\n\nThe analyses in \\S\\ref{sec:confirmation-bias}--\\S\\ref{sec:remediation} confirm all three predictions of the Symbolic Mirage hypothesis (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}): self-extracted constraints inherit the generating model's bias. The oracle gap (\\S\\ref{sec:oracle}) confirms the mechanism is sound given uncontaminated constraints; the corrective signal must come from outside the model's distribution.\n\n\\paragraph{Method.}\nWe test this with DeBERTa-large \\citep{he2021deberta} (406M, encoder-only), fine-tuned on MultiNLI \\citep{williams2018mnli}, classifying (premise, conclusion) pairs into \\textsc{entailment}, \\textsc{contradiction}, or \\textsc{neutral}. In combo mode, the NLI verdict is integrated with SC's $K$ votes via weight $w$: each NLI-supporting vote counts $w$ times. We evaluate on Mistral-7B FOLIO ($n = 204$) and ProofWriter depth-5 ($n = 600$), because NLI classification requires (premise, conclusion) pairs that these datasets naturally provide; multiple-choice datasets (LogiQA) lack this structure.\n\n\\paragraph{Main result.}\nDeBERTa NLI alone achieves 49.51\\% on FOLIO (below SC's 55.88\\%) and 55.00\\% on ProofWriter (above SC's 39.33\\%). On FOLIO ($n = 204$), combo mode yields $+1.96$\\,pp at $w{=}1$ and $+2.94$\\,pp at $w{=}3$ (both n.s.). On ProofWriter ($n = 600$), $w{=}3$ achieves $+3.50$\\,pp ($p = 0.013$) and $w{=}5$ reaches $+10.67$\\,pp ($p < 0.001$)---while same-model SICA \\emph{degrades} ProofWriter ($-2.83$\\,pp, $p = 0.014$; Table~\\ref{tab:main_results}), illustrating that corrective signal must originate outside the model's distribution (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). We report $w{=}3$ as primary; higher weights risk overfitting. After Benjamini--Hochberg correction ($m{=}6$), ProofWriter $w{=}3$ remains significant (adjusted $p = 0.038$) and $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$); FOLIO comparisons are non-significant. Per-label caveat: DeBERTa over-predicts \\textsc{False} on ProofWriter (67.8\\%) and performs poorly on \\textsc{Unknown} (3.5\\% accuracy), as NLI's ``neutral'' fails to capture logical unknowability.\n\n\\paragraph{Why it works.}\nThe 406M encoder-only DeBERTa and 7B decoder-only Mistral share no architecture, training data, or inference mechanism, satisfying the independence condition that same-model sampling cannot provide (\\S\\ref{sec:mirage}). This produces per-label complementarity: NLI excels on \\textsc{False} problems (71.43\\% vs.\\ SC's 38.10\\%) where the LLM is weakest, while SC dominates \\textsc{Unknown} (68.12\\% vs.\\ 18.84\\%). All three NLI architectures tested---DeBERTa, RoBERTa, and BART (all MNLI-trained)---produce significant improvements on ProofWriter at $w \\geq 3$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:multi-nli}), confirming the effect is not verifier-specific.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability gap hypothesis.}\nCross-architecture verification succeeds when the verifier's standalone accuracy exceeds the generator's SC baseline. On ProofWriter, DeBERTa standalone (55.00\\%) exceeds Mistral-7B SC (39.33\\%), enabling $+10.67$\\,pp at $w{=}5$ ($p < 0.001$). On Qwen3-14B FOLIO, where SC (86.27\\%) far exceeds DeBERTa (49.51\\%), the combo yields $\\Delta \\leq +0.49$\\,pp (all $p = 1.0$). Aggregation gain shrinks with model capability: $+8.01$\\,pp (Mistral-7B) to $+0.86$\\,pp (Qwen3-14B thinking mode; Appendix~\\ref{app:single-trace}). Across all conditions at $w{=}3$, combo gain tracks the capability gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:capability-gap}): meaningful improvement emerges only when the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's SC baseline.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.82\\columnwidth]{figures/capability_gap_scatter}\n\\vspace{-3mm}\n\\caption{Combo gain vs.\\ capability gap (verifier standalone $-$ generator SC) at $w{=}3$. Meaningful gain emerges when the gap is positive (verifier exceeds generator).}\n\\label{fig:capability-gap}\n\\vspace{-6mm}\n\\end{figure}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

Three of four Mistral-7B verifiers produce significant gains ($p < 0.001$), while neither Qwen3-14B condition reaches significance. The pattern is consistent: NLI escape is strongest when the generator is weak (SC $= 39.33\\%$) and the verifier's standalone accuracy approaches or exceeds the generator's baseline. When the generator is strong (SC $= 85.00\\%$), even architecturally independent verifiers cannot contribute corrective signal, confirming that the capability gap is the binding constraint rather than architectural diversity alone.",

    "snippet": "1\tSelf-Consistency (SC) majority voting over chain-of-thought traces saturates as sample count grows because same-model traces are correlated voters.\n2\tWe investigate whether symbolic constraint extraction can break this plateau through Symbolic Invariant Consensus Aggregation (SICA), which extracts logical constraints from reasoning traces and selects answers via weighted partial MAX-SAT.\n3\tAcross six open-source LLMs (7B--32B, from Mistral-7B at 54\\% SC to Qwen3-14B thinking mode at 86\\% SC) and three logical reasoning domains with over 3,000 condition-evaluations (${\\sim}$1,250 unique instances), we find that self-extracted constraints do not improve majority voting at any capacity level: none of sixteen non-degenerate conditions shows a significant positive effect; TOST equivalence tests (margin $\\pm 2$\\,pp) establish equivalence for four conditions, while five show genuine SICA--SC differences---four of which are negative.\n4\tWe identify \\emph{symbolic confirmation bias} as the root cause: on incorrect problems, 83.3\\% of self-extracted constraints support the wrong majority answer (bias ratio $4.97\\times$), and 94\\% of errors overlap with SC.\n5\tProcess-level analysis reveals a deeper bottleneck: chain-of-thought cosine similarity ($0.69$) far exceeds answer-level Fleiss' $\\kappa$ ($0.41$), indicating that traces share reasoning processes even when they diverge on answers.\n6\tTwenty-plus same-model remediation strategies spanning fifteen families all fail, revealing a persistent independence-accuracy trade-off at the 7B--32B scale: methods that increase trace independence destroy accuracy (Debate: $\\kappa = 0.15$, $-18.6$\\,pp), while methods preserving accuracy cannot break the correlation floor ($\\kappa \\geq 0.40$ across three model families).\n7\tOracle controls confirm the aggregation mechanism is sound ($+29.90$\\,pp and $+14.22$\\,pp on FOLIO, $+11.50$\\,pp ProofWriter; $p < 0.001$); same-model self-extraction captures only ${\\sim}$12\\% of the oracle ceiling.\n8\tHowever, a cross-architecture proof-of-concept---DeBERTa-large NLI combined with SC---yields $+3.50$\\,pp on ProofWriter ($p = 0.013$; $+10.67$\\,pp at $w{=}5$, $p < 0.001$), confirmed across three NLI architectures, suggesting that the oracle gap is partially recoverable when verification signal comes from outside the reasoning model's distribution."
  },

I can condense the experiments section to reference the analysis section instead, which would free up meaningful space without losing information. Type C conditions with low semantic coverage show the opposite pattern—SICA actively degrades performance by formalizing flawed reasoning, as seen with Mistral-7B where the approach significantly hurts accuracy. When baseline agreement is extremely low, SICA essentially collapses to the original semantic coverage by design. Running equivalence tests with a ±2 percentage point margin, I find that only a minority of conditions show genuine differences between SICA and semantic coverage, with most negative effects outweighing the rare positive ones.

In the intermediate semantic coverage range around 50–55%, results become more mixed and seed-dependent—some runs show modest improvements while others stagnate, and even identical baseline conditions can diverge substantially depending on the random seed, suggesting that the quality of individual reasoning traces matters more than the aggregate statistics.

For high-performing models (SC ≥ 60%), formalization provides no meaningful gains and sometimes causes harm, as seen with Qwen2.5-14B where it actually degrades performance. Conversely, in the low-performance regime (SC ≤ 40%), SICA can actively hurt by formalizing incorrect reasoning, and when the correct-reasoning fraction drops below 5%, the method collapses to the baseline by design.

Four supporting analyses confirm this mechanism...
